# Front Cover

->[Binding Description]<-

This manuscript is bound in dark brown leather, showing the characteristic wear of several centuries, including staining and small holes likely caused by insects <note>These are known as "wormholes," common in antique leather and wood</note>. 

The cover is decorated with **blind tooling** <term>blind tooling: a technique where decorative patterns are pressed into leather without using gold leaf or color</term>. The design features:

*   An outer rectangular border featuring a repeating **interlace** or "knotwork" pattern.
*   A central decorative frame in the shape of a semicircular arch, also filled with intricate knotwork.
*   Small, delicate floral stamps placed at the base of the arch and within the corners of the inner frame.

<margin>Vatican Apostolic Library</margin> <note>The modern digital watermark indicates this volume is currently held in the Vatican collections.</note>

<summary>The front cover of a historical leather-bound manuscript featuring traditional blind-tooled interlace patterns and a central arched motif.</summary>
<keywords>binding, leather, blind tooling, interlace, knotwork, Vatican Library</keywords>

and resembles the voice of the heavenly hosts. And the other Sunday,

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory material to the New Testament epistles, specifically concluding a note on the Epistle to Philemon and beginning an explanation of the manuscript's scholarly apparatus. The Armenian page number is 43 (մգ).</meta>

... [entirety] and the orders of the church.

<margin>43</margin>
<margin>104</margin>
**H**e writes the Letter to Philemon concerning the believing servant Onesimus, who previously was useless, but later, having turned from those former ways, became worthy of freedom through the intercession <note>original: *barekhōsut‘iwn*</note> of the apostle. Furthermore, he also became a witness for Christ <note>original abbreviation *K'i* stands for *K'ristosi*</note> in the city of the Romans there, before the lord Tertullus <note>Armenian: *Tightos*; likely referring to a Roman official or perhaps a corruption of a name like Tertullus or Titus in the context of Onesimus’s hagiography</note> who held such authority of a chiliarch <term>chiliarch: a military commander of a thousand soldiers</term>. With patience, he fulfilled the verdict of martyrdom through high-shattering blows.

**A**nd thus, all these books contain various examples of virtue for spiritual growth. Something shall be said by us briefly regarding souls, and for each of the Letters, one by one, we shall arrange the preface before the chapters—a division which our wise and God-loving fathers have labored upon. And not only that, but also the established divisions of the readings, and all the testimonies of the Holy Scriptures and the aids to understanding <note>literally "easy-makers"</note>. We shall include the learned interpretations eloquently spoken by us, so that by following the uniform reading, we shall place them alongside each and every preface...

<summary>This page concludes a brief introduction to the Epistle to Philemon, focusing on the transformation and martyrdom of the servant Onesimus, and begins an explanation of the manuscript's organizational features, such as prefaces and chapter divisions.</summary>
<keywords>Epistle to Philemon, Onesimus, martyrdom, chiliarch, Tertullus, biblical apparatus, prefaces, Holy Scripture</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory section of the manuscript, transitioning from the general plan for the biblical commentary to a specific chronological verification of the Apostle Paul’s ministry based on historical records. It provides a timeline from the Crucifixion of Christ to the conversion of Paul.</meta>

-> ...of the explanations. <-

<margin>÷</margin>But it was considered important to briefly verify the time of Paul’s preaching by taking a summary from the *Chronological Canons* <term>Chronological Canons: a famous historical timeline written by the early church historian Eusebius of Caesarea</term> of Eusebius of Pamphilus <note>original: *Ewsebi Pamp'ighloy*</note>. Therefore, having taken the book itself and opened it, I found that the sufferings of our Savior occurred in the eighteenth year of Emperor Tiberius. And immediately following, on the third day, the Resurrection; and again, on the fortieth day, the ascent of Christ <note>original abbreviation *K'i* for *K'ristosi*</note> into heaven. And after a few days, I saw there the selection of the Apostles for ministry, and Stephen himself, the namesake <note>original: *anuanakirn*; referring to Stephen's name meaning "crown" or "crowned one"</note>, and others who were his fellow-branches <note>literally "saplings"</note> with him. And after this, I saw a great mob of Jews descending upon them, just as we mentioned further above. And Stephen contended <note>original: *paterazmeal*; used here in the sense of a spiritual or martyrological struggle</note> there, with Paul consenting to the killing; who, after a short while, approached the rulers of the Jews, took a letter, and was carrying it to the Jews in Damascus regarding the disciples. And in the middle of the way, there was a voice to him from God <note>original abbreviation *yAy* for *yAstutsoy*</note>, and it entered into...

<summary>The author uses Eusebius of Caesarea’s chronological records to establish a timeline for the early Church, linking the crucifixion of Christ in the 18th year of Tiberius to the martyrdom of Stephen and the beginning of Paul’s journey to Damascus.</summary>
<keywords>Apostle Paul, Eusebius of Caesarea, Chronological Canons, Emperor Tiberius, Saint Stephen, Damascus, Resurrection, Ascension</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the chronological account of the Apostle Paul’s ministry. Having established his conversion in the previous passage, the text now details his travels, his imprisonment under the governors Felix and Festus, and his eventual appeal to Rome.</meta>

...in that same year. At the beginning of the nineteenth year of Emperor Tiberius, the history says the preaching of Paul the Apostle began. And traveling through the whole world, he preached the true <term>worship of God: Armenian *astvats-pashtut'yun*, referring to true religion or piety</term>, until the thirteenth year of Emperor Claudius. During the governorship of Felix over the Jews, before whom Paul, being accused by the Jews, made his defense. He was held bound in prison in Caesarea for two years. After which, Porcius Festus, having succeeded him in authority, immediately desired to judge Paul, considering it a great favor <note>literally "a great benefit" or "grace"</note> to the Jews. Then the blessed one <note>referring to St. Paul</note> realized that he could not escape that conspiracy in any other way unless he appealed to the emperor’s court. And then from there he was sent to Rome to Emperor Nero. Aristarchus was with him, whom he appropriately calls his fellow prisoner <note>original: *gerekits iwr*</note> in one of his letters. And Luke, who wrote and handed down the Acts of the Apostles in books, was also there. And from there, to the Romans' city—

<summary>This page outlines the timeline of St. Paul’s missionary work and legal struggles, specifically his two-year imprisonment in Caesarea and his transfer to Rome to stand trial before Nero.</summary>
<keywords>St. Paul, Emperor Tiberius, Claudius, Felix, Porcius Festus, Nero, Aristarchus, St. Luke, Caesarea, Rome, Acts of the Apostles</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Apostle Paul’s arrival in Rome and his legal proceedings before Emperor Nero. The previous page concluded with Paul being sent to the capital city accompanied by Aristarchus and Luke.</meta>

...stayed there again for two years—

<margin>Paul was held</margin>
Luke tells of these things and up to this point in the *Acts of the Apostles*, a book which he authored at that very time. He did not know subsequent events with such certainty, and he did not record the testimony <note>The word *vkayut'yun* often refers specifically to "martyrdom" in early Christian texts</note> of Paul in his writings, for Luke and Aristarchus left him there and departed. However, Eusebius <note>referring to the historian Eusebius of Caesarea, c. 260–339 AD</note> recounted to us the events that followed, having researched them accurately. In the second volume of his *Church History*, he provides testimony concerning him, saying that Paul traveled freely. He notes that Paul preached the word of God <gloss>God</gloss> without hindrance. At that time, Paul stood before Nero’s tribunal and made his own defense; he then returned to the service of preaching, having been released by the emperor. He continued to spread the Gospel for another two years.

But Nero, having reached the height of his depravity <note>literally "the head of his delusion"</note>, first murdered his mother Agrippina, then his own sister, then his wife Octavia, and many others among his own kinsfolk.

<summary>This page transition focuses on the conclusion of the biblical Acts of the Apostles and cites the historian Eusebius to fill in the gaps of Paul’s later life, including his release from his first Roman imprisonment before the onset of Nero’s infamously violent reign.</summary>

<keywords>St. Paul, St. Luke, Acts of the Apostles, Eusebius of Caesarea, Church History, Emperor Nero, Agrippina, Octavia, martyrdom, Roman history</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Apostle Paul’s final days in Rome, transitioning from Nero’s personal crimes to his state-sponsored persecution of the early Church. It draws upon the historical chronology of Roman Emperors to date Paul’s martyrdom.</meta>

...they considered <note>The text continues from the previous page describing Nero's descent into madness</note>, but he stirred up persecutions against the Christians. Then, intending the murder of the apostles, he summoned Paul and once again brought him before the tribunal. Luke was with him again. And there Paul reached the thirty-sixth year after the life-giving <term>Passion</term> <note>referring to the crucifixion and suffering of Jesus; original: *charcharanats*</note> of the Lord, and in the thirteenth year of Emperor Nero, having borne witness <note>The term *vkayeal* literally means "testified," but in early Christian literature, it specifically refers to "martyrdom"</note>, the head of the blessed one was cut off by the sword.

<margin>7 —</margin> Now, it was from the nineteenth year of Emperor Tiberius <note>Emperor Tiberius reigned 14–37 AD</note>—at which time he began to preach the Gospel—until his [Tiberius's] twenty-second year: four years. And for Gaius <note>Emperor Caligula, reigned 37–41 AD</note> likewise four years. Again, and Claudius <note>Emperor Claudius, reigned 41–54 AD</note> for slightly less than fourteen years. Nero succeeded him, and in the thirteenth year of his own reign, he killed the apostle.

<margin>7 —</margin> Now Paul the Apostle himself writes to Timothy regarding his first defense, and he speaks thus: “At my first defense, no one stood with me, but all deserted me; may it not be counted against them. But the Lord...” <note>Quoting 2 Timothy 4:16. The text breaks off as Paul begins to describe how God strengthened him.</note>

<summary>This page describes the martyrdom of the Apostle Paul by beheading during the 13th year of Nero's reign and provides a regnal chronology of Roman Emperors to date Paul's ministry. It concludes with a quote from Paul’s second letter to Timothy regarding his lonely legal defense in Rome.</summary>

<keywords>St. Paul, St. Luke, Emperor Nero, Tiberius, Gaius Caligula, Claudius, martyrdom, beheading, 2 Timothy, Roman chronology, Church history</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the quote from the Apostle Paul’s second letter to Timothy (4:17), which was introduced at the end of the previous page. It concludes the narrative of Paul’s legal defense and provides a chronological summary of his ministry.</meta>

...stood by me and strengthened me, so that the preaching might be confirmed through me and all the nations might hear; and I was delivered from the mouth of the lion <note>original: *aritsun*; this refers to the Apostle Paul’s survival of his first trial under Emperor Nero, who is allegorically called the lion</note>. And he spoke this regarding Nero. But regarding the second [trial] in which he was to meet his end according to the testimony <note>The term *vkayutyun* (testimony) is used here in its technical sense of martyrdom</note>, he says: "I have finished the course of the good service, for I am henceforth devoted, and the time of my return <note>original: *dartsi*; here meaning his departure from life or return to God</note> has arrived for me." And because Luke was with him at once, and a little while later from here in Rome, [he writes]: "Luke greets you."

<margin>2</margin> **A**nd now, the entire duration of Paul’s preaching was eleven years <note>The text uses Armenian numerals *Zh. ev mi* (11). However, the subsequent list (11 + 2 + 2 + 10) sums to 25, while the author concludes the total is 35. It is likely the first number was intended to be 21</note>, and another two years which he completed in the prison in Caesarea, and again among the Romans, another two first years in Rome, and then ten years [more]. Thus, the total for him altogether, from his beginning until the completion, was thirty-five years.

**B**ut let no one be an accuser or find fault with what I am saying, [arguing] that in the Acts of the Apostles by Luke, these things are not said. O man...

<sig>2</sig>

<summary>This page concludes Paul's reflections on his trials under Nero, quoting 2 Timothy to distinguish between his first release and his final martyrdom. The author also provides a chronological breakdown of Paul's 35-year ministry, defending his calculations against those who notice discrepancies with the biblical Book of Acts.</summary>

<keywords>Apostle Paul, Nero, Luke the Evangelist, 2 Timothy, Caesarea, Rome, chronology of Paul, martyrdom, Acts of the Apostles</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the historical gap in the biblical Book of Acts. The author is explaining why the physician Luke did not record the death of the Apostle Paul and is justifying the use of other historical traditions to complete the narrative of Paul’s life and martyrdom.</meta>

...should you remain one who asks [this]. If, because it is not written in the Acts of the Apostles, you do not accept it, and say, “Bring Luke here, that he may tell of Paul’s testimony <note>original: *vkayut’iun*; here meaning his martyrdom</note>.” If Luke had ended the account of Paul’s life in Rome after only those two years <note>referring to the conclusion of the Book of Acts, which ends with Paul under house arrest for two years</note>, and had told us of his martyrdom, then we would have no need to investigate the chronology. 

But he did not write of his martyrdom here. Agree then with the sequence of the chronicler, and receive this history with a loving and attentive mind. For by the consent and faith of Christ’s disciples, having received the teaching and traditions of the fathers, they become 

<margin><image-desc>A decorative red cross or cruciform symbol with some flourishes.</image-desc></margin>

heirs in the edifice of the Kingdom of Heaven.

### <unclear>The Martyrdom of Paul the Apostle</unclear>

**U**nder Nero, the Roman Emperor, the Apostle Paul gave his testimony there. His head was cut off with a sword on the thirtieth day of the month, in the third year after the life-giving Sufferings <note>original: *charcharanats*; a reference to the Passion of Christ, used here as a liturgical or era-marker</note>. He fought the good fight in the city of Rome; on the fifth day...

<summary>The author explains that the Book of Acts ends before Paul's death, necessitating the use of other traditions to document his martyrdom. It concludes with a biographical summary of Paul’s execution by sword under Emperor Nero.</summary>
<keywords>Apostle Paul, Luke the Evangelist, Nero, martyrdom, Rome, Acts of the Apostles, chronology, Church tradition</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical and chronological record of the Apostle Paul's life, transitioning from the narrative of his death to a scholarly account of the textual history and organization of his letters.</meta>

...of the month of Panemos. Three days before the Calends <term>Calends: the first day of the month in the Roman calendar</term> of the month of July, which is the month of Margats <note>original: *Margats*; the tenth month of the ancient Armenian calendar</note>.
<margin>⌞</margin> on which the holy Apostle reached his end through his testimony <note>original: *vkayut’iun*; here meaning his martyrdom</note>, in the sixty-ninth year of our Savior Jesus Christ, which is the Palestinian [reckoning]. And now, the total time from when Paul was martyred is three hundred and thirty years, until the fourth consulship of Arcadius and the third consulship of Honorius—the two brothers reigning as Augusti <term>Augusti: the title for senior Roman Emperors</term>—in the ninth of Dios <note>original: *Dioskonii*; a month in the Macedonian calendar</note>, in the fifteenth cycle of years, in the month of June, which is Mareri <note>original: *Mareri*; another name for the Armenian month Margats</note>, which was the nineteenth day of the month. 
<margin>⊕</margin> I have accurately recorded the time of the Apostle Paul’s martyrdom, the full contents of the readings and the chapters within them, and the citations <note>original: *vkayut’iunk’*; referential proofs or testimonies</note> according to each of the Apostle’s epistles, and the number of verses <note>original: *tunk’*; literally 'houses,' here meaning lines of scripture</note> belonging to each of these.

<margin>Chapter 2</margin> For there is no partiality <note>original: "aknarutyun," literally "looking at the face," meaning favoritism or respect of persons.</note> before <term>God<note>original: "ay," abbreviation for "Astutsoy."</note></term>. For as many as have sinned without the law shall also perish without the law; and as many as have sinned under the law shall be judged by the law. For it is not the hearers of the law who are righteous before <term>God<note>original: "ay," abbreviation for "Astutsoy."</note></term>, but the doers of the law who shall be justified. 

But when the Gentiles <note>original: "hetanosk," referring to non-Jewish nations who did not receive the Mosaic Law.</note>, who do not have the law, do by nature the things of the law, these, not having the law, are a law unto themselves. They show the work of the law written in their hearts, with the testimony of their minds <note>original: "vkayutp mtats," often translated as "conscience."</note> and their thoughts reproving or perhaps even defending one another on the day when <term>God<note>original: "ats," abbreviation for "Astvats."</note></term> shall judge the secrets of men according to my gospel through <term>Jesus Christ<note>original: "yi ki," abbreviation for "Hisusi Kristosi."</note></term>.

...infants, having the form of knowledge and truth in the law. Now then, you who teach others, do you not teach yourself? You who preach against stealing, do you steal? <margin>Isaiah 40 <note>The scribe cites Isaiah 40, though the quotation "the name of God is blasphemed..." is actually found in Isaiah 52:5.</note></margin>
For the name of <term>God<note>original: "ay," abbreviation for "Astutsoy."</note></term> is blasphemed among the Gentiles because of you, just as it is written. Circumcision <note>The physical sign of the covenant between God and the descendants of Abraham.</note> is indeed useful if you keep the law; but if you are a breaker of the law, your circumcision has become uncircumcision. 

So if the uncircumcised man <note>Referring to a Gentile who has not undergone the ritual.</note> keeps the requirements of the law, will not his uncircumcision be counted as circumcision? And will not the one who is physically uncircumcised, by fulfilling the law, judge you who, despite the written law and circumcision, are a transgressor of the law? For a person is not a Jew who is one outwardly, nor is circumcision something outward in the flesh. Rather, a person is a Jew who is one inwardly; and circumcision is of the heart—by the Spirit and not by the written letter. Such a person’s praise is not from men, but from <term>God<note>original: "yastutsoy."</note></term>.

And he received the sign of <term>circumcision<note>original: "thlphatutyun," the physical ritual marking the covenant with God.</note></term> as a seal of the <term>righteousness<note>original: "ardarutyun," meaning to be in a right relationship with God.</note></term> of the faith he had while in <term>uncircumcision<note>original: "yanthlphatutenen."</note></term>. And he is the father of circumcision not only to those who are circumcised, but also to those who walk in the footsteps of the faith our father Abraham had while he was still uncircumcised.

For the <term>promise<note>original: "avetik," which can mean "good news" or "glad tidings," here referring specifically to God's promise to Abraham.</note></term> to Abraham and his descendants—that he would be the heir of the world—was not through the law, but through the righteousness of faith. For if those who are of the law are the heirs, then faith is made empty and the promise is made void. For the law brings about wrath; but where there is no law, there is also no transgression.

Because of this, it is by faith, so that it might be according to grace, in order that the promise might be made firm to all his descendants—not only to those who are of the law, but also to those who are of the faith of Abraham, <margin>17th of <term>Genesis<note>original: "Yararatsots," the Book of Genesis.</note></term></margin> who is the father of us all. As it is written: “I have made you a father of many nations.”

**...you are Abraham’s offspring, heirs according to the promise.**

I say this: as long as the heir is a child <note>The Armenian "tghay" refers to a minor or an infant, someone not yet of legal age to claim an inheritance.</note>, he is no different than a servant, even though he is the lord of all. Instead, he is under stewards and governors <note>original: "հազարապետաւք" (hazarapetawk) and "գաւառապետաւք" (gawarapetawk) — historical titles for administrators and regional prefects.</note> until the time appointed by the father. In the same way, while we were children, we were held in bondage under the elements of this world. But when the fullness of time had come, <term>God|original abbreviation: "ած" (astuats)</term> sent his Son, born of a woman and born under the law, to redeem those who were under the law, so that we might receive the adoption as sons. And because you are sons, <term>God</term> sent the Spirit of his Son into our hearts, crying out: **Abba, Father** <note>original: "աբբա հայր" (abba hayr). "Abba" is an Aramaic word for "father," used here to show a new, intimate relationship with the divine.</note>. So you are no longer a servant, but a son; and if a son, then an heir of <term>God|original abbreviation: "այ" (astuatsoy)</term>. But at that time, when you did not know <term>God</term>, you served those who by nature are not <term>gods|original abbreviation: "ածք" (astuatst'k)</term>. But now that you have known <term>God</term>, or rather, have been known by <term>God</term>, how is it that you turn back again to the weak and beggarly elements, to which [you desire] again...

<margin>Chapter 2</margin>
**I** want you to know what a great struggle I have within myself for you and for those in Laodicea <note>An ancient city in Asia Minor, near Colossae</note>, and for all those who have not seen my face in person. I do this so that their hearts may be encouraged, being brought together in love and attaining all the riches of the full assurance of wisdom and the knowledge of the mystery <term>mystery (խորհուրդ): a divine truth once hidden but now revealed to believers</term> of God in Christ Jesus. In Him are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge. I say this so that no one may deceive you with persuasive or flowery speech. For even though I am far away in the body, I am with you in spirit. I rejoice as I see your orderly conduct and the firmness of your faith in Christ Jesus. Therefore, just as you received Christ Jesus the Lord, continue to live your lives in Him—rooted and built up in Him, and established in the faith just as you were taught, abounding in it with thanksgiving.

# On Truth and the Resurrection

For truly, without hesitation, the ray of truth does not shine <note>original: "ճառագայտիլ զկանձն ճշմարտութի"</note> upon the soul; rather, it is only from the impure and from evil mixtures that they might advance further into impiety. And like a bitter weed <note>likely a reference to the "tares" or "dross" mentioned in biblical parables</note>, it devours the treasure. For this reason, do not be deceived by those who are glorified in their arrogance and vanity, claiming that the resurrection from sin has already occurred. They have overthrown the faith of some; yet the foundation of the Lord stands firm. If the soul has recognized the eternal path which is its own, then let its whole self be dedicated to eternity without end.

But in a great house <note>A reference to 2 Timothy 2:20</note> there are not only vessels of gold and silver, but also of wood and clay. And regarding their own resurrection, if they do not wake up for that purpose, then from that great fear, the honorable vessel shall become useless and decay.

<meta>This page marks a significant transition in the manuscript. # Testimony concerning our Lord Jesus
## Christ from the Book of the Vision of John
### the Evangelist: The beginning and
### the word-for-word explanation of the commentary

->**The Revelation of Jesus Christ,**<-
->**through which God granted him to show**<-
->**unto his servants**<-

That which must shortly come to pass; the testimony of Jesus Christ which he saw; and what was, and what was to come after that. Grace be unto you and peace, from the One Who Is <note>original: "յէէն" (y-ē-ēn), a philosophical and literal Armenian translation of the Divine Name, "The Being" or "The One who truly exists"</note>, and who Was, and who is to Come; and from the sevenfold spirit <note>original: "յեաութէ" (yeaut'ē), likely a variant for the seven spirits of God mentioned in Revelation 1:4</note> which is before His throne; and from Jesus Christ, the faithful witness, who is the firstborn from the dead, and the ruler of the kingdoms of the earth.

He who loved us and loosed the bonds <note>original: "kapans" (կապանս), meaning chains or shackles</note> of our sins by his blood, and made us kings and priests to his Father; to Him be glory and power forever and ever. Amen.

Behold, he is coming with the clouds of heaven. And every eye shall see him, and those who <note>The Armenian text breaks off here at the word "who" (որք), reflecting the bottom of the manuscript page; the verse would conclude with "and those who pierced him."</note>

->**The Revelation of John**<-

--- Page 348 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page, completing verse 7 of the first chapter of the Book of Revelation. This manuscript page transitions from the opening salutation of the Apocalypse into John’s first vision of the glorified Christ, interspersed with brief theological commentary.</meta>

<margin>
Rev. 
Verse 7
</margin>
pierced him. And all the tribes of the earth shall mourn because of him. Amen, Amen. I am the **Alpha** and the **Omega**. Amen, says the Lord God <note>original: "ած" (ats), a standard abbreviation for "Astvats" (God)</note>, Who Is, and Who Is <note>The Armenian transcription repeats "Who Is" (որ էն), though the subsequent commentary suggests the second should be "Who Was" (որ էրն)</note>, and Who is to come, the Lord Almighty.

**Revelation** is said to be the manifestation of a hidden mystery. **"Who Is"** reveals the Father, who said to Moses, "I am Who Is." **"Who Was"** refers to the Word <note>The Logos, or Christ</note>, according to that: "In the beginning was the Word." And **"Who is to come"** refers to the Comforter <note>The Holy Spirit</note>, about whom he later spoke regarding Christ.

I was in the Spirit on the **Lord's day** <note>literally "the day of Sunday"</note>. And I heard a great voice as of a trumpet, saying: I am the **Alpha** and I am the **Omega**, the first and the last. And I turned and saw **seven golden lampstands**; and in the midst of the lampstands one like the Son <note>referring to the "Son of Man"</note>, clothed in an ornate robe <term>pchnavor: a long, decorative garment reaching the feet, often associated with high-priestly or royal status</term>, and girded about the breasts with a golden girdle. When I saw him, I fell at his feet as though dead. And he laid his right hand upon me, and said: Fear not. I am the beginning and I am the end. I am the **Life**, and I am he who died; and behold, I am alive forever and ever. And I have the keys of death and of Hades...

---

[concluded from previous page] ...Constantinople. <note>original: "ստանդնուպօլիս" (standnupōlis); the end of the word for Constantinople, suggesting the previous page discussed the city's symbolic role.</note> And the woman cares for that tabernacle <note>The "tabernacle" or tent here symbolizes a temporary dwelling of worldly indulgence.</note> because of the seven <note>original: "է" (ē); the Armenian letter used as the numeral 7.</note> primary pleasures that are within her. And regarding the scarlet beast, it is evident that it refers to the slanderer <note>original: "բանսարկուն" (bansarkun); a traditional title for the Devil as the "accuser."</note> who is pleased by the drinking of blood and the pleasure of sins. And it is full of blasphemy: that is to say, because of frequent visions [pursued] without fear, many heresies arose; for the beginning of wisdom is the fear [of God].

<margin>Rev. 17:6-8</margin>
**A**nd I saw the woman drunk with the blood of the saints and with the blood of the witnesses <note>original: "վկայիցն" (vkayitsn); often translated as martyrs, those who bear witness unto death.</note>. And the beast that was and is not, and is about to come up from the abyss <note>original: "դժոխոց" (dzhokhots); literally "hells," referring here to the bottomless pit.</note> and go to perdition.

The beast is the slanderer <note>the Devil</note>, who prowls around seeking someone to devour. He was slain by the Cross, and again at the end of time he will come to life <note>This refers to the "healing of the mortal wound" mentioned in Revelation.</note>. And he performs signs and works of deception, in order to deny the Savior. And the "was" refers to the time before the Cross, and "is not" refers to today, after the Cross. Again, he awakens in the times of the Antichrist <term>Antichrist</term> <note>original: "նեռինն" (nerinn).</note>, and after that, he goes to eternal perdition.

<margin>Rev. 18:1</margin>
**A**fter this I saw another angel coming down from heaven, who had great authority, and this earth was illuminated by his glory. And he cried out with a loud voice:

->....<-
->Of John:<- <note>original: "Յովհաննու" (Yovhannu); likely referring to the Gospel/Revelation of John or the commentator.</note>

<summary>This page provides a theological commentary on the scarlet beast of Revelation, identifying it as the Devil whose power was broken by the Cross but will be revived by the Antichrist. It transitions into the vision of the angel in Revelation 18 whose glory illuminates the earth.</summary>

**B**abylon the great has fallen, which had become a dwelling of demons. Rejoice over her, O heavens, and holy apostles, and prophets, for God has judged the Lord’s judgment <note>original: "զդատաստանն Տեր" (zdatastann Ter); This implies that God has vindicated the cause of the righteous by passing sentence on their persecutor.</note> against her.

<margin>
151
</margin>
**T**his angel was among the highest of the first ranks, and the brightness of the angels was more than the rays of the luminaries <note>The sun, moon, and stars.</note>. The angel cried out the fall of the city. And regarding the sins that were the cause of punishment, heaven rejoices—that is to say, the ranks of the heavenly beings—and all the saints, who see their vengeance <note>In this context, "vengeance" refers to the restoration of divine justice rather than personal spite.</note>.

<margin>
152
</margin>
**A**fter this I heard a great voice of a multitude in heaven, saying: Hallelujah! Salvation and glory and honor and power to our God, for truly His judgments are [just]. And again they said: Hallelujah! Because God our Almighty has reigned; let us be glad and rejoice and give glory to Him, for the marriage of the Lamb has come.

**H**allelujah signifies lordship <note>original: "տէրութիւն" (tērut‘iwn); While the Hebrew "Hallelujah" literally means "Praise the Lord," Armenian commentators often glossed it as a declaration of God's sovereignty or "The Lord has appeared."</note>, and "Amen" means "so be it." And the marriage [is] the union <note>original: "միաւորու-" (miavoru-); The word is truncated at the line break, likely intended to be "union" (miavorut‘iwn).</note> of the Church...

<summary>This page interprets the fall of Babylon and the heavenly praise that follows, explaining the high status of the announcing angel and defining the liturgical terms Hallelujah and Amen within the context of the Marriage of the Lamb.</summary>

->Union with Christ<-

<margin><image-desc>Decorative cross containing the Armenian numeral 16</image-desc></margin>
**H**is wife, the bride, has made herself
ready. And it was given to her to clothe herself
in fine linen—white, holy, and shining.
And one of the elders said: Blessed are
the fallen asleep <note>Those who have died in the faith.</note> who are called to the marriage 
supper of the Lamb.

---

<margin><image-desc>Decorative cross containing the Armenian numeral 17</image-desc></margin>
**T**he Church displays her radiant virtue
and their holy mind. The Kingdom is
sometimes called "Paradise"; sometimes "Abraham’s side" <note>original: "գունդ արբահամու" (gund arbahamu); literally "Abraham's troop" or "gathering," a common Armenian reference to "Abraham's Bosom," the resting place of the righteous.</note>;
sometimes "a banquet" and "a wedding," because of the 
inexpressible joy. But that [Kingdom] 
surpasses this [world] as much as light surpasses 
darkness, and peace surpasses suffering.
